From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF25.9, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because at work it’s been getting hard to explain things. I’m a supervisor in retail, so I’m supposed to talk through the inventory numbers, the weekend schedule, who’s covering what, trucks coming in, all of that. But when I start explaining it, my words kind of… jump around.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to tell someone about the inventory count, and then I’m talking about the truck delivery, and then Thursday, and then somehow I mention the church grill or the way my boss said something, and I can see people’s faces change. They look at me like they’re trying to follow me but they can’t. And I know I had a point when I started, but it gets tangled up coming out.\n\nIt’s been bothering me because I’m used to being the one who keeps things organized, you know? But lately when I talk, it doesn’t come out organized. That’s really why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently started or increased your use of any substances, such as alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can think of. I mean, I’ll have a drink here and there, like if there’s a family thing or dinner or something, but I haven’t started anything new or increased anything. No drugs. Nothing like that.\n\nI know people ask that because it can make your head feel different or make you talk different, but I don’t think that’s what’s going on with me. At least, there hasn’t been some change like that where I’d say, “Oh, that started after I began using something.” I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing difficulties with disorganized thinking or speech in situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does happen outside work too, but work is where it shows the most because I’m expected to explain things clearly. Like with inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll start in one place and then suddenly I’m talking about the truck coming in, then Thursday, then something about the church grill, then my boss’s tone of voice, and people just kind of look at me like they can’t follow the road I’m on.\n\nBut it’s not only at work. It’s more noticeable there because there are numbers and schedules and people waiting for instructions. I’ll be trying to say something simple, and it feels like my words take side streets. In my head it feels connected at the time, but when I see the other person’s face, I realize maybe I skipped steps or mixed things together. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to be confusing. I’m trying to explain it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that other people don't?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the way people usually mean, like hearing a voice talking to me when nobody’s there, or seeing a person standing in the room that other people can’t see. I can’t say that’s been happening.\n\nSometimes I’ll mishear something at work, like if there’s noise in the store or the radio is on or people are talking over each other, and I might think someone called my name, but then I look around and it’s just the overhead music or someone talking in the next aisle. But I think that’s normal, or at least it feels explainable to me.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed hearing or seeing things other people don’t. My issue is more… when I’m trying to say things, it gets all tangled. But hearing or seeing things, no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overly anxious or stressed when you're unable to express yourself clearly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I get pretty stressed by it, because it’s not like I don’t know what I’m trying to say. That’s the part that bothers me. I’ll have the point in my head, like inventory numbers or who’s supposed to be on the weekend schedule, and then when I start explaining it out loud, it kind of jumps around on me.\n\nI’ll start with the numbers, then suddenly I’m talking about the truck coming in, and then Thursday, and then somehow I’m mentioning the church grill or my boss’s tone, and I can see people’s faces change. They’re trying to follow me, but they can’t, and then I notice that, and that makes me more anxious, so I try to explain it again, but sometimes that just makes it worse.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I came in because of that kind of thing. It’s not just being nervous about speaking. It’s more like I can’t keep the road straight when I’m talking, and then I feel pressure because people are waiting for me to make sense. That stress builds up fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or actions that seem disorganized or hard to follow, similar to how you described your speech?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not as obvious to me as the talking thing, because with speech people react right away, you know, they stop me or they look confused. But with my actions, I’ll notice it after a minute and think, “What am I doing here?”\n\nLike on the sales floor, sometimes I’ll find myself pacing the same aisle more than once, like back and forth, not really getting anywhere. Or I’ll be straightening the same row of batteries over and over, even though they’re already straight. I’ll fix them, step away, come back, fix them again, and it’s not like that’s the most important thing happening.\n\nAnd then there are times I’ll be standing there with the schedule clipboard in my hand, and the associates are waiting for me to tell them where to go or what to do next, and I’m just… stuck for a bit. I know I’m supposed to be giving direction, but I’m not moving into the next step. It feels like I’m holding the information but not turning it into an action.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to be supervising, keeping things organized, making the plan clear. But sometimes my behavior probably looks scattered too, not just my words.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of disorganized speech and behavior happen all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go, mostly. It’s not every single minute of every day, but it’s been happening more than it used to, if that makes sense.\n\nI’d say this has been building for… about 11 months now. I remember it pretty clearly because it was around when the new district manager came in and started pushing harder on forecasts and schedules. Before that I might get flustered sometimes, sure, but I could usually keep myself in line. Since then, it feels like the pressure sort of exposed something, or made it worse—I don’t know exactly how to say it.\n\nSome days I can get through most of my shift and I’m only a little tangled up. Other days, especially when I’m trying to explain staffing or numbers or timing, it starts coming out wrong and then my actions get kind of scattered too. So it’s not constant, but it’s been regular enough over these months that I can’t really call it a one-time thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad, hopeless, or depressed when you're not struggling to express yourself or when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I get worn down by all of this, and when I’m away from work I can still think about how badly something went or worry that people noticed, but if you’re asking whether I feel sad or hopeless when I’m just at home and not trying to explain something… I don’t know that I’ve noticed it in that clear way.\n\nIt’s more like I feel tired of dealing with it, maybe discouraged sometimes, because it makes me question myself. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there feeling hopeless for no reason. I think most of the bad feeling comes after I’ve gotten tangled up or after something at work, and then I replay it. Outside of that, I’m not sure. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t think I’ve clearly noticed, like, “Oh, I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s not that obvious to me.\n\nThere are things I don’t get around to as much, but I don’t know if that’s because I’ve lost interest or because I’m tired, or distracted, or just kind of preoccupied with how things are going. Like, if I’m home, sometimes I’ll intend to watch something or do something I normally like, and then I end up just sitting there thinking for a while. But I don’t know if that counts as not enjoying it.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say yes confidently. I haven’t really tracked it that way. It’s more that everything feels harder to settle into sometimes, but I don’t know that the interest itself is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a significant level of anxiety or fear when you're not having trouble with your thoughts or speech?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s a significant level, no. I mean, I do get uneasy sometimes, but I have a hard time separating out what’s anxiety from just… being keyed up after dealing with people all day, or worrying that I’m going to mess something up again.\n\nWhen I’m not in the middle of having trouble with my thoughts or words, I don’t think I’m walking around scared all the time. It’s more like there’s a background tension sometimes, especially if I know I have to talk through something complicated later. But if I’m just sitting at home or doing something ordinary, I don’t usually feel fear in a big obvious way.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed anxiety as its own separate thing. I can get nervous, sure, but I don’t know that I’d call it significant unless it’s connected to worrying about my speech or how I’m coming across.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen or heard things that weren't really there when you were alone?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least, not in a way I could confidently point to and say, “That wasn’t there.” When I’m alone, sometimes I’ll hear normal house noises or something outside and my mind kind of focuses on it too much, but I don’t know that I’ve heard a voice or seen something that I knew couldn’t be real.\n\nI guess I’m a little hesitant because sometimes, especially if I’m tired, I can misread things for a second—like a shadow or a noise—and then I look again or listen and it’s just something ordinary. But that seems different from actually seeing or hearing things that aren’t there. So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed that happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days when your symptoms are so bad that you can't do your job or take care of your daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’d have to say yes, though I don’t like saying it that plainly. It’s not like I just stay in bed and don’t show up at all, but it has definitely gotten in the way.\n\nAt work, I’m a first-line supervisor, so I’m supposed to be able to keep things moving, especially when the store is busy. And weekends used to be kind of my thing, or at least something they trusted me with. But lately they don’t really put me in charge of the busy weekend shifts anymore. That was hard to ignore, because nobody says it in a dramatic way, but you can tell. It’s like they’re trying to route around me.\n\nAnd after my last performance review, my boss basically warned me that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. So that’s… I mean, that’s not just me being sensitive. That’s a real consequence.\n\nAt home it’s been happening too. My wife has had to step in for things I used to handle, like commitments with other people or situations where I’m supposed to be the one talking or organizing something. People have gotten uncomfortable or confused around me, and then she ends up smoothing it over or just doing it herself. I feel bad about that, because I don’t want her carrying things that are supposed to be mine.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I can technically get through parts of the day, but I’m not really functioning the way I’m supposed to. It’s affecting work and home more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic, happy, or irritable, and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re describing, at least.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I’ve been wound up or irritable, especially if something at work is hanging over me or if I feel like people are watching for me to make a mistake. And there are nights where I don’t sleep well because I’m replaying things in my head. But that’s different from feeling extremely energetic or happy, like I’m on top of everything, or like I don’t need sleep.\n\nI’ve never really had a stretch where I was sleeping only a little and still felt great or unusually powerful or anything like that. If I don’t sleep, I usually feel worse the next day, not better. More tired, more on edge, more likely to have trouble getting through things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of period. At least nothing that stands out to me as unusual in that direction.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in how much you enjoy the things you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t know that I’ve noticed it clearly, like, “Oh, I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more complicated than that, I guess.\n\nThere are things I still like in theory, or I remember liking them, but sometimes when I’m actually doing them I’m distracted or preoccupied, so it’s hard to tell whether the enjoyment is gone or whether I’m just not fully there for it. Like if I’m watching something or spending time at home, I might not settle into it the way I used to, but I wouldn’t say I feel nothing.\n\nSo… maybe a little? But I’m hesitant to call it a big decrease. I haven’t been tracking it that way. I think I still can enjoy things, just maybe not as easily when my mind is occupied.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're unable to perform your job or daily tasks as well as you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t like admitting it, because I’ve always seen myself as somebody who could handle the job, you know? I’m a first-line supervisor, so people are supposed to be able to come to me, and I’m supposed to keep things organized when it gets busy.\n\nBut I can tell things have changed. They don’t really trust me with the busy weekend shifts anymore, not the way they used to. Nobody sits me down and says, “We don’t think you can do this,” exactly, but you notice when schedules change and when someone else is put in charge of the shifts you used to run. It feels pretty obvious after a while.\n\nAnd then after my performance review, my boss warned me that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. So that made it feel very real. It wasn’t just me wondering if I was slipping — it was being said to me in a work setting, with consequences attached.\n\nAt home too, my wife has had to cover for me more than before. There are commitments I used to handle, or situations where I would normally talk to people or keep things moving, and now she ends up stepping in because people seem uncomfortable or confused around me. That part bothers me a lot, because I don’t want to put her in that position.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’m not doing my job or daily responsibilities as well as I used to. I can still do some things, but not at the level people expected from me before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant decrease in your ability to interact with others or perform daily tasks compared to before these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I mean, that’s probably one of the main ways I can tell something has changed, because it’s not just how I feel inside — other people are reacting to it now.\n\nAt work, I used to be trusted with the busy weekend shifts. Those were kind of the shifts where you had to keep everything moving, deal with customers, keep the staff organized, make decisions quickly. And now… they don’t really put me in that position the same way. It’s not always said directly, but you notice when responsibilities get shifted away from you. After my performance review, my boss told me pretty clearly that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended, so that was hard to ignore.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has had to step in more than she used to. There are commitments I used to handle myself — talking to people, keeping plans straight, dealing with situations — and now sometimes she ends up covering for me because people seem uncomfortable or confused around me. I can see it happening, and it’s embarrassing. I don’t want her to have to smooth things over for me.\n\nSo yes, compared to before, I’d say my ability to deal with people and keep up with daily responsibilities has definitely gone down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced seeing or hearing things that you knew were not real, even if it was just for a moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t think I’ve had something where I could clearly say, “Yes, I saw a thing and knew it wasn’t real,” or “I heard a voice and knew it wasn’t there.” Not in a clean way like that.\n\nSometimes I second-guess things, like whether I heard someone say something or whether I misread a sound in the house or at work, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being “completely disconnected from the world.” That sounds more extreme than what I’d know how to say yes to.\n\nI haven’t had moments where I felt like the world wasn’t real or like I wasn’t actually there, if that’s what you mean. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been totally cut off from everything around me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But sometimes my behavior probably looks scattered too, not just my words.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of disorganized speech and behavior happen all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go, mostly. It’s not every single minute of every day, but it’s been happening more than it used to, if that makes sense.\n\nI’d say this has been building for… about 11 months now. I remember it pretty clearly because it was around when the new district manager came in and started pushing harder on forecasts and schedules. Before that I might get flustered sometimes, sure, but I could usually keep myself in line. Since then, it feels like the pressure sort of exposed something, or made it worse—I don’t know exactly how to say it.\n\nSome days I can get through most of my shift and I’m only a little tangled up. Other days, especially when I’m trying to explain staffing or numbers or timing, it starts coming out wrong and then my actions get kind of scattered too. So it’s not constant, but it’s been regular enough over these months that I can’t really call it a one-time thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad, hopeless, or depressed when you're not struggling to express yourself or when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I get worn down by all of this, and when I’m away from work I can still think about how badly something went or worry that people noticed, but if you’re asking whether I feel sad or hopeless when I’m just at home and not trying to explain something… I don’t know that I’ve noticed it in that clear way.\n\nIt’s more like I feel tired of dealing with it, maybe discouraged sometimes, because it makes me question myself. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there feeling hopeless for no reason. I think most of the bad feeling comes after I’ve gotten tangled up or after something at work, and then I replay it. Outside of that, I’m not sure. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t think I’ve clearly noticed, like, “Oh, I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s not that obvious to me.\n\nThere are things I don’t get around to as much, but I don’t know if that’s because I’ve lost interest or because I’m tired, or distracted, or just kind of preoccupied with how things are going. Like, if I’m home, sometimes I’ll intend to watch something or do something I normally like, and then I end up just sitting there thinking for a while. But I don’t know if that counts as not enjoying it.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say yes confidently. I haven’t really tracked it that way. It’s more that everything feels harder to settle into sometimes, but I don’t know that the interest itself is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a significant level of anxiety or fear when you're not having trouble with your thoughts or speech?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s a significant level, no. I mean, I do get uneasy sometimes, but I have a hard time separating out what’s anxiety from just… being keyed up after dealing with people all day, or worrying that I’m going to mess something up again.\n\nWhen I’m not in the middle of having trouble with my thoughts or words, I don’t think I’m walking around scared all the time. It’s more like there’s a background tension sometimes, especially if I know I have to talk through something complicated later. But if I’m just sitting at home or doing something ordinary, I don’t usually feel fear in a big obvious way.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed anxiety as its own separate thing. I can get nervous, sure, but I don’t know that I’d call it significant unless it’s connected to worrying about my speech or how I’m coming across.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen or heard things that weren't really there when you were alone?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least, not in a way I could confidently point to and say, “That wasn’t there.” When I’m alone, sometimes I’ll hear normal house noises or something outside and my mind kind of focuses on it too much, but I don’t know that I’ve heard a voice or seen something that I knew couldn’t be real.\n\nI guess I’m a little hesitant because sometimes, especially if I’m tired, I can misread things for a second—like a shadow or a noise—and then I look again or listen and it’s just something ordinary. But that seems different from actually seeing or hearing things that aren’t there. So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed that happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days when your symptoms are so bad that you can't do your job or take care of your daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’d have to say yes, though I don’t like saying it that plainly. It’s not like I just stay in bed and don’t show up at all, but it has definitely gotten in the way.\n\nAt work, I’m a first-line supervisor, so I’m supposed to be able to keep things moving, especially when the store is busy. And weekends used to be kind of my thing, or at least something they trusted me with. But lately they don’t really put me in charge of the busy weekend shifts anymore. That was hard to ignore, because nobody says it in a dramatic way, but you can tell. It’s like they’re trying to route around me.\n\nAnd after my last performance review, my boss basically warned me that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. So that’s… I mean, that’s not just me being sensitive. That’s a real consequence.\n\nAt home it’s been happening too. My wife has had to step in for things I used to handle, like commitments with other people or situations where I’m supposed to be the one talking or organizing something. People have gotten uncomfortable or confused around me, and then she ends up smoothing it over or just doing it herself. I feel bad about that, because I don’t want her carrying things that are supposed to be mine.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I can technically get through parts of the day, but I’m not really functioning the way I’m supposed to. It’s affecting work and home more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic, happy, or irritable, and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re describing, at least.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I’ve been wound up or irritable, especially if something at work is hanging over me or if I feel like people are watching for me to make a mistake. And there are nights where I don’t sleep well because I’m replaying things in my head. But that’s different from feeling extremely energetic or happy, like I’m on top of everything, or like I don’t need sleep.\n\nI’ve never really had a stretch where I was sleeping only a little and still felt great or unusually powerful or anything like that. If I don’t sleep, I usually feel worse the next day, not better. More tired, more on edge, more likely to have trouble getting through things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of period. At least nothing that stands out to me as unusual in that direction.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in how much you enjoy the things you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t know that I’ve noticed it clearly, like, “Oh, I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more complicated than that, I guess.\n\nThere are things I still like in theory, or I remember liking them, but sometimes when I’m actually doing them I’m distracted or preoccupied, so it’s hard to tell whether the enjoyment is gone or whether I’m just not fully there for it. Like if I’m watching something or spending time at home, I might not settle into it the way I used to, but I wouldn’t say I feel nothing.\n\nSo… maybe a little? But I’m hesitant to call it a big decrease. I haven’t been tracking it that way. I think I still can enjoy things, just maybe not as easily when my mind is occupied.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're unable to perform your job or daily tasks as well as you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t like admitting it, because I’ve always seen myself as somebody who could handle the job, you know? I’m a first-line supervisor, so people are supposed to be able to come to me, and I’m supposed to keep things organized when it gets busy.\n\nBut I can tell things have changed. They don’t really trust me with the busy weekend shifts anymore, not the way they used to. Nobody sits me down and says, “We don’t think you can do this,” exactly, but you notice when schedules change and when someone else is put in charge of the shifts you used to run. It feels pretty obvious after a while.\n\nAnd then after my performance review, my boss warned me that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. So that made it feel very real. It wasn’t just me wondering if I was slipping — it was being said to me in a work setting, with consequences attached.\n\nAt home too, my wife has had to cover for me more than before. There are commitments I used to handle, or situations where I would normally talk to people or keep things moving, and now she ends up stepping in because people seem uncomfortable or confused around me. That part bothers me a lot, because I don’t want to put her in that position.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’m not doing my job or daily responsibilities as well as I used to. I can still do some things, but not at the level people expected from me before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant decrease in your ability to interact with others or perform daily tasks compared to before these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I mean, that’s probably one of the main ways I can tell something has changed, because it’s not just how I feel inside — other people are reacting to it now.\n\nAt work, I used to be trusted with the busy weekend shifts. Those were kind of the shifts where you had to keep everything moving, deal with customers, keep the staff organized, make decisions quickly. There are commitments I used to handle myself — talking to people, keeping plans straight, dealing with situations — and now sometimes she ends up covering for me because people seem uncomfortable or confused around me. I can see it happening, and it’s embarrassing. I don’t want her to have to smooth things over for me.\n\nSo yes, compared to before, I’d say my ability to deal with people and keep up with daily responsibilities has definitely gone down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced seeing or hearing things that you knew were not real, even if it was just for a moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t think I’ve had something where I could clearly say, “Yes, I saw a thing and knew it wasn’t real,” or “I heard a voice and knew it wasn’t there.” Not in a clean way like that.\n\nSometimes I second-guess things, like whether I heard someone say something or whether I misread a sound in the house or at work, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I mean, people hear noises and then realize it was the TV or someone in the next room, so I don’t want to make too much out of normal stuff.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that in a definite way. If you mean actual seeing or hearing things that I knew weren’t real, I can’t really point to an example.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were completely disconnected from the world around you?\n\nPatient: Completely disconnected… I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly.\n\nThere are times where I feel kind of distant or not fully engaged, like I’m sitting there and things are happening around me but I’m not really keeping up with the rhythm of it. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being “completely disconnected from the world.” That sounds more extreme than what I’d know how to say yes to.\n\nI haven’t had moments where I felt like the world wasn’t real or like I wasn’t actually there, if that’s what you mean. No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where things feel stressful or kind of foggy, like I’m not fully following what’s going on around me, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling like I’m in a dream or like the room or people around me aren’t real.

But hearing or seeing things, no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overly anxious or stressed when you're unable to express yourself clearly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I get pretty stressed by it, because it’s not like I don’t know what I’m trying to say. That’s the part that bothers me. I’ll have the point in my head, like inventory numbers or who’s supposed to be on the weekend schedule, and then when I start explaining it out loud, it kind of jumps around on me.\n\nI’ll start with the numbers, then suddenly I’m talking about the truck coming in, and then Thursday, and then somehow I’m mentioning the church grill or my boss’s tone, and I can see people’s faces change. They’re trying to follow me, but they can’t, and then I notice that, and that makes me more anxious, so I try to explain it again, but sometimes that just makes it worse.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I came in because of that kind of thing. It’s not just being nervous about speaking. It’s more like I can’t keep the road straight when I’m talking, and then I feel pressure because people are waiting for me to make sense. That stress builds up fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or actions that seem disorganized or hard to follow, similar to how you described your speech?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not as obvious to me as the talking thing, because with speech people react right away, you know, they stop me or they look confused. But with my actions, I’ll notice it after a minute and think, “What am I doing here?”\n\nLike on the sales floor, sometimes I’ll find myself pacing the same aisle more than once, like back and forth, not really getting anywhere. Or I’ll be straightening the same row of batteries over and over, even though they’re already straight. I’ll fix them, step away, come back, fix them again, and it’s not like that’s the most important thing happening.\n\nAnd then there are times I’ll be standing there with the schedule clipboard in my hand, and the associates are waiting for me to tell them where to go or what to do next, and I’m just… stuck for a bit. I know I’m supposed to be giving direction, but I’m not moving into the next step. It feels like I’m holding the information but not turning it into an action.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to be supervising, keeping things organized, making the plan clear. But sometimes my behavior probably looks scattered too, not just my words.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of disorganized speech and behavior happen all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go, mostly. It’s not every single minute of every day, but it’s been happening more than it used to, if that makes sense.\n\nI’d say this has been building for… about 11 months now. I remember it pretty clearly because it was around when the new district manager came in and started pushing harder on forecasts and schedules. Before that I might get flustered sometimes, sure, but I could usually keep myself in line. Since then, it feels like the pressure sort of exposed something, or made it worse—I don’t know exactly how to say it.\n\nSome days I can get through most of my shift and I’m only a little tangled up. Other days, especially when I’m trying to explain staffing or numbers or timing, it starts coming out wrong and then my actions get kind of scattered too. So it’s not constant, but it’s been regular enough over these months that I can’t really call it a one-time thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad, hopeless, or depressed when you're not struggling to express yourself or when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I get worn down by all of this, and when I’m away from work I can still think about how badly something went or worry that people noticed, but if you’re asking whether I feel sad or hopeless when I’m just at home and not trying to explain something… I don’t know that I’ve noticed it in that clear way.\n\nIt’s more like I feel tired of dealing with it, maybe discouraged sometimes, because it makes me question myself. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there feeling hopeless for no reason. I think most of the bad feeling comes after I’ve gotten tangled up or after something at work, and then I replay it. Outside of that, I’m not sure. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t think I’ve clearly noticed, like, “Oh, I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s not that obvious to me.\n\nThere are things I don’t get around to as much, but I don’t know if that’s because I’ve lost interest or because I’m tired, or distracted, or just kind of preoccupied with how things are going. Like, if I’m home, sometimes I’ll intend to watch something or do something I normally like, and then I end up just sitting there thinking for a while. But I don’t know if that counts as not enjoying it.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say yes confidently. I haven’t really tracked it that way. It’s more that everything feels harder to settle into sometimes, but I don’t know that the interest itself is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel a significant level of anxiety or fear when you're not having trouble with your thoughts or speech?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s a significant level, no. I mean, I do get uneasy sometimes, but I have a hard time separating out what’s anxiety from just… being keyed up after dealing with people all day, or worrying that I’m going to mess something up again.\n\nWhen I’m not in the middle of having trouble with my thoughts or words, I don’t think I’m walking around scared all the time. It’s more like there’s a background tension sometimes, especially if I know I have to talk through something complicated later. But if I’m just sitting at home or doing something ordinary, I don’t usually feel fear in a big obvious way.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed anxiety as its own separate thing. I can get nervous, sure, but I don’t know that I’d call it significant unless it’s connected to worrying about my speech or how I’m coming across.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen or heard things that weren't really there when you were alone?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least, not in a way I could confidently point to and say, “That wasn’t there.” When I’m alone, sometimes I’ll hear normal house noises or something outside and my mind kind of focuses on it too much, but I don’t know that I’ve heard a voice or seen something that I knew couldn’t be real.\n\nI guess I’m a little hesitant because sometimes, especially if I’m tired, I can misread things for a second—like a shadow or a noise—and then I look again or listen and it’s just something ordinary. But that seems different from actually seeing or hearing things that aren’t there. So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed that happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days when your symptoms are so bad that you can't do your job or take care of your daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’d have to say yes, though I don’t like saying it that plainly. It’s not like I just stay in bed and don’t show up at all, but it has definitely gotten in the way.\n\nAt work, I’m a first-line supervisor, so I’m supposed to be able to keep things moving, especially when the store is busy. And weekends used to be kind of my thing, or at least something they trusted me with. But lately they don’t really put me in charge of the busy weekend shifts anymore. That was hard to ignore, because nobody says it in a dramatic way, but you can tell. It’s like they’re trying to route around me.\n\nAnd after my last performance review, my boss basically warned me that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. So that’s… I mean, that’s not just me being sensitive. That’s a real consequence.\n\nAt home it’s been happening too. My wife has had to step in for things I used to handle, like commitments with other people or situations where I’m supposed to be the one talking or organizing something. People have gotten uncomfortable or confused around me, and then she ends up smoothing it over or just doing it herself. I feel bad about that, because I don’t want her carrying things that are supposed to be mine.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I can technically get through parts of the day, but I’m not really functioning the way I’m supposed to. It’s affecting work and home more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic, happy, or irritable, and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re describing, at least.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I’ve been wound up or irritable, especially if something at work is hanging over me or if I feel like people are watching for me to make a mistake. And there are nights where I don’t sleep well because I’m replaying things in my head. But that’s different from feeling extremely energetic or happy, like I’m on top of everything, or like I don’t need sleep.\n\nI’ve never really had a stretch where I was sleeping only a little and still felt great or unusually powerful or anything like that. If I don’t sleep, I usually feel worse the next day, not better. More tired, more on edge, more likely to have trouble getting through things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of period. At least nothing that stands out to me as unusual in that direction.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in how much you enjoy the things you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t know that I’ve noticed it clearly, like, “Oh, I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more complicated than that, I guess.\n\nThere are things I still like in theory, or I remember liking them, but sometimes when I’m actually doing them I’m distracted or preoccupied, so it’s hard to tell whether the enjoyment is gone or whether I’m just not fully there for it. Like if I’m watching something or spending time at home, I might not settle into it the way I used to, but I wouldn’t say I feel nothing.\n\nSo… maybe a little? But I’m hesitant to call it a big decrease. I haven’t been tracking it that way. I think I still can enjoy things, just maybe not as easily when my mind is occupied.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're unable to perform your job or daily tasks as well as you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t like admitting it, because I’ve always seen myself as somebody who could handle the job, you know? I’m a first-line supervisor, so people are supposed to be able to come to me, and I’m supposed to keep things organized when it gets busy.\n\nBut I can tell things have changed. They don’t really trust me with the busy weekend shifts anymore, not the way they used to. Nobody sits me down and says, “We don’t think you can do this,” exactly, but you notice when schedules change and when someone else is put in charge of the shifts you used to run. It feels pretty obvious after a while.\n\nAnd then after my performance review, my boss warned me that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. So that made it feel very real. It wasn’t just me wondering if I was slipping — it was being said to me in a work setting, with consequences attached.\n\nAt home too, my wife has had to cover for me more than before. There are commitments I used to handle, or situations where I would normally talk to people or keep things moving, and now she ends up stepping in because people seem uncomfortable or confused around me. That part bothers me a lot, because I don’t want to put her in that position.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’m not doing my job or daily responsibilities as well as I used to. I can still do some things, but not at the level people expected from me before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant decrease in your ability to interact with others or perform daily tasks compared to before these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I mean, that’s probably one of the main ways I can tell something has changed, because it’s not just how I feel inside — other people are reacting to it now.\n\nAt work, I used to be trusted with the busy weekend shifts. Those were kind of the shifts where you had to keep everything moving, deal with customers, keep the staff organized, make decisions quickly. And now… they don’t really put me in that position the same way. It’s not always said directly, but you notice when responsibilities get shifted away from you. After my performance review, my boss told me pretty clearly that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended, so that was hard to ignore.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has had to step in more than she used to. There are commitments I used to handle myself — talking to people, keeping plans straight, dealing with situations — and now sometimes she ends up covering for me because people seem uncomfortable or confused around me. I can see it happening, and it’s embarrassing. I don’t want her to have to smooth things over for me.\n\nSo yes, compared to before, I’d say my ability to deal with people and keep up with daily responsibilities has definitely gone down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced seeing or hearing things that you knew were not real, even if it was just for a moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t think I’ve had something where I could clearly say, “Yes, I saw a thing and knew it wasn’t real,” or “I heard a voice and knew it wasn’t there.” Not in a clean way like that.\n\nSometimes I second-guess things, like whether I heard someone say something or whether I misread a sound in the house or at work, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I mean, people hear noises and then realize it was the TV or someone in the next room, so I don’t want to make too much out of normal stuff.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that in a definite way. If you mean actual seeing or hearing things that I knew weren’t real, I can’t really point to an example.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were completely disconnected from the world around you?\n\nPatient: Completely disconnected… I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly.\n\nThere are times where I feel kind of distant or not fully engaged, like I’m sitting there and things are happening around me but I’m not really keeping up with the rhythm of it. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being “completely disconnected from the world.” That sounds more extreme than what I’d know how to say yes to.\n\nI haven’t had moments where I felt like the world wasn’t real or like I wasn’t actually there, if that’s what you mean. Mostly it’s more that I get overwhelmed or I sort of withdraw into myself, and then later I realize I wasn’t responding the way people expected me to. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been totally cut off from everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were living in a dream or that things around you weren't real?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where things feel stressful or kind of foggy, like I’m not fully following what’s going on around me, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling like I’m in a dream or like the room or people around me aren’t real.\n\nWhen you say “not real,” I think of something more dramatic, like looking at my wife or my workplace and feeling like it’s fake or staged or like I’m asleep, and I can’t really say that’s happened. I might feel distracted, or like I’m having trouble keeping up, but the world still feels like the world, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… no, not in that dreamlike way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. They don’t really trust me with the busy weekend shifts anymore, not the way they used to. Nobody sits me down and says, “We don’t think you can do this,” exactly, but you notice when schedules change and when someone else is put in charge of the shifts you used to run. It feels pretty obvious after a while.\n\nAnd then after my performance review, my boss warned me that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. So that made it feel very real. It wasn’t just me wondering if I was slipping — it was being said to me in a work setting, with consequences attached.\n\nAt home too, my wife has had to cover for me more than before. There are commitments I used to handle, or situations where I would normally talk to people or keep things moving, and now she ends up stepping in because people seem uncomfortable or confused around me. That part bothers me a lot, because I don’t want to put her in that position.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’m not doing my job or daily responsibilities as well as I used to. I can still do some things, but not at the level people expected from me before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant decrease in your ability to interact with others or perform daily tasks compared to before these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I mean, that’s probably one of the main ways I can tell something has changed, because it’s not just how I feel inside — other people are reacting to it now.\n\nAt work, I used to be trusted with the busy weekend shifts. Those were kind of the shifts where you had to keep everything moving, deal with customers, keep the staff organized, make decisions quickly. And now… they don’t really put me in that position the same way. It’s not always said directly, but you notice when responsibilities get shifted away from you. After my performance review, my boss told me pretty clearly that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended, so that was hard to ignore.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has had to step in more than she used to. There are commitments I used to handle myself — talking to people, keeping plans straight, dealing with situations — and now sometimes she ends up covering for me because people seem uncomfortable or confused around me. I can see it happening, and it’s embarrassing. I don’t want her to have to smooth things over for me.\n\nSo yes, compared to before, I’d say my ability to deal with people and keep up with daily responsibilities has definitely gone down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced seeing or hearing things that you knew were not real, even if it was just for a moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t think I’ve had something where I could clearly say, “Yes, I saw a thing and knew it wasn’t real,” or “I heard a voice and knew it wasn’t there.” Not in a clean way like that.\n\nSometimes I second-guess things, like whether I heard someone say something or whether I misread a sound in the house or at work, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I mean, people hear noises and then realize it was the TV or someone in the next room, so I don’t want to make too much out of normal stuff.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that in a definite way. If you mean actual seeing or hearing things that I knew weren’t real, I can’t really point to an example.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were completely disconnected from the world around you?\n\nPatient: Completely disconnected… I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly.\n\nThere are times where I feel kind of distant or not fully engaged, like I’m sitting there and things are happening around me but I’m not really keeping up with the rhythm of it. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being “completely disconnected from the world.” That sounds more extreme than what I’d know how to say yes to.\n\nI haven’t had moments where I felt like the world wasn’t real or like I wasn’t actually there, if that’s what you mean. Mostly it’s more that I get overwhelmed or I sort of withdraw into myself, and then later I realize I wasn’t responding the way people expected me to. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been totally cut off from everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were living in a dream or that things around you weren't real?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where things feel stressful or kind of foggy, like I’m not fully following what’s going on around me, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling like I’m in a dream or like the room or people around me aren’t real.\n\nWhen you say “not real,” I think of something more dramatic, like looking at my wife or my workplace and feeling like it’s fake or staged or like I’m asleep, and I can’t really say that’s happened. I might feel distracted, or like I’m having trouble keeping up, but the world still feels like the world, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… no, not in that dreamlike way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know that I’d say I no longer enjoy things. That sounds very absolute to me, like nothing gives me any pleasure anymore, and I’m not sure that’s true.

I mean, some things don’t land the same way they used to, maybe, but I also wonder if that’s just getting older or being tired or having too much on my mind. Like if I watch something or do something I normally like, sometimes I’m not as into it as I expected to be, but other times I can still enjoy it. So I have trouble knowing where the line is.

I remember it pretty clearly because it was around when the new district manager came in and started pushing harder on forecasts and schedules. Before that I might get flustered sometimes, sure, but I could usually keep myself in line. Since then, it feels like the pressure sort of exposed something, or made it worse—I don’t know exactly how to say it.\n\nSome days I can get through most of my shift and I’m only a little tangled up. Other days, especially when I’m trying to explain staffing or numbers or timing, it starts coming out wrong and then my actions get kind of scattered too. I mean, I do get uneasy sometimes, but I have a hard time separating out what’s anxiety from just… being keyed up after dealing with people all day, or worrying that I’m going to mess something up again.\n\nWhen I’m not in the middle of having trouble with my thoughts or words, I don’t think I’m walking around scared all the time. It’s more like there’s a background tension sometimes, especially if I know I have to talk through something complicated later. But if I’m just sitting at home or doing something ordinary, I don’t usually feel fear in a big obvious way.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed anxiety as its own separate thing. I can get nervous, sure, but I don’t know that I’d call it significant unless it’s connected to worrying about my speech or how I’m coming across.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen or heard things that weren't really there when you were alone?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least, not in a way I could confidently point to and say, “That wasn’t there.” When I’m alone, sometimes I’ll hear normal house noises or something outside and my mind kind of focuses on it too much, but I don’t know that I’ve heard a voice or seen something that I knew couldn’t be real.\n\nI guess I’m a little hesitant because sometimes, especially if I’m tired, I can misread things for a second—like a shadow or a noise—and then I look again or listen and it’s just something ordinary. My wife has had to step in for things I used to handle, like commitments with other people or situations where I’m supposed to be the one talking or organizing something. People have gotten uncomfortable or confused around me, and then she ends up smoothing it over or just doing it herself. I feel bad about that, because I don’t want her carrying things that are supposed to be mine.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I can technically get through parts of the day, but I’m not really functioning the way I’m supposed to. It’s affecting work and home more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely energetic, happy, or irritable, and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re describing, at least.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I’ve been wound up or irritable, especially if something at work is hanging over me or if I feel like people are watching for me to make a mistake. And there are nights where I don’t sleep well because I’m replaying things in my head. But that’s different from feeling extremely energetic or happy, like I’m on top of everything, or like I don’t need sleep.\n\nI’ve never really had a stretch where I was sleeping only a little and still felt great or unusually powerful or anything like that. If I don’t sleep, I usually feel worse the next day, not better. More tired, more on edge, more likely to have trouble getting through things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of period. At least nothing that stands out to me as unusual in that direction.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in how much you enjoy the things you used to like doing?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t know that I’ve noticed it clearly, like, “Oh, I don’t enjoy anything anymore.” It’s more complicated than that, I guess.\n\nThere are things I still like in theory, or I remember liking them, but sometimes when I’m actually doing them I’m distracted or preoccupied, so it’s hard to tell whether the enjoyment is gone or whether I’m just not fully there for it. Like if I’m watching something or spending time at home, I might not settle into it the way I used to, but I wouldn’t say I feel nothing.\n\nSo… maybe a little? But I’m hesitant to call it a big decrease. I haven’t been tracking it that way. I think I still can enjoy things, just maybe not as easily when my mind is occupied.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're unable to perform your job or daily tasks as well as you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t like admitting it, because I’ve always seen myself as somebody who could handle the job, you know? I’m a first-line supervisor, so people are supposed to be able to come to me, and I’m supposed to keep things organized when it gets busy.\n\nBut I can tell things have changed. They don’t really trust me with the busy weekend shifts anymore, not the way they used to. Nobody sits me down and says, “We don’t think you can do this,” exactly, but you notice when schedules change and when someone else is put in charge of the shifts you used to run. It feels pretty obvious after a while.\n\nAnd then after my performance review, my boss warned me that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. So that made it feel very real. It wasn’t just me wondering if I was slipping — it was being said to me in a work setting, with consequences attached.\n\nAt home too, my wife has had to cover for me more than before. There are commitments I used to handle, or situations where I would normally talk to people or keep things moving, and now she ends up stepping in because people seem uncomfortable or confused around me. That part bothers me a lot, because I don’t want to put her in that position.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’m not doing my job or daily responsibilities as well as I used to. I can still do some things, but not at the level people expected from me before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant decrease in your ability to interact with others or perform daily tasks compared to before these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I mean, that’s probably one of the main ways I can tell something has changed, because it’s not just how I feel inside — other people are reacting to it now.\n\nAt work, I used to be trusted with the busy weekend shifts. Those were kind of the shifts where you had to keep everything moving, deal with customers, keep the staff organized, make decisions quickly. And now… they don’t really put me in that position the same way. It’s not always said directly, but you notice when responsibilities get shifted away from you. After my performance review, my boss told me pretty clearly that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended, so that was hard to ignore.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has had to step in more than she used to. There are commitments I used to handle myself — talking to people, keeping plans straight, dealing with situations — and now sometimes she ends up covering for me because people seem uncomfortable or confused around me. I can see it happening, and it’s embarrassing. I don’t want her to have to smooth things over for me.\n\nSo yes, compared to before, I’d say my ability to deal with people and keep up with daily responsibilities has definitely gone down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced seeing or hearing things that you knew were not real, even if it was just for a moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I don’t think I’ve had something where I could clearly say, “Yes, I saw a thing and knew it wasn’t real,” or “I heard a voice and knew it wasn’t there.” Not in a clean way like that.\n\nSometimes I second-guess things, like whether I heard someone say something or whether I misread a sound in the house or at work, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I mean, people hear noises and then realize it was the TV or someone in the next room, so I don’t want to make too much out of normal stuff.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had that in a definite way. If you mean actual seeing or hearing things that I knew weren’t real, I can’t really point to an example.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were completely disconnected from the world around you?\n\nPatient: Completely disconnected… I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly.\n\nThere are times where I feel kind of distant or not fully engaged, like I’m sitting there and things are happening around me but I’m not really keeping up with the rhythm of it. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being “completely disconnected from the world.” That sounds more extreme than what I’d know how to say yes to.\n\nI haven’t had moments where I felt like the world wasn’t real or like I wasn’t actually there, if that’s what you mean. Mostly it’s more that I get overwhelmed or I sort of withdraw into myself, and then later I realize I wasn’t responding the way people expected me to. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been totally cut off from everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were living in a dream or that things around you weren't real?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had times where things feel stressful or kind of foggy, like I’m not fully following what’s going on around me, but I wouldn’t describe it as feeling like I’m in a dream or like the room or people around me aren’t real.\n\nWhen you say “not real,” I think of something more dramatic, like looking at my wife or my workplace and feeling like it’s fake or staged or like I’m asleep, and I can’t really say that’s happened. I might feel distracted, or like I’m having trouble keeping up, but the world still feels like the world, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… no, not in that dreamlike way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I no longer enjoy things. That sounds very absolute to me, like nothing gives me any pleasure anymore, and I’m not sure that’s true.\n\nI mean, some things don’t land the same way they used to, maybe, but I also wonder if that’s just getting older or being tired or having too much on my mind. Like if I watch something or do something I normally like, sometimes I’m not as into it as I expected to be, but other times I can still enjoy it. So I have trouble knowing where the line is.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear loss of enjoyment across the board. It’s more… I might be less enthusiastic sometimes, or it takes more to get me interested, but I can’t confidently say, “Yes, I don’t enjoy pleasurable activities anymore.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
